# English Form 5, Form Five English: authored answers for review

Every answer fixed by a person, not computed. Part 1: the tables, conventions and rules that computed answers come from.
Part 2: every authored question template, with all its data (keys, statements, pairs, choices and feedback) and sources.
Regenerate with `npm run review:f5-english`. Item IDs (TR-…) match `docs/teacher-review.md`.

## Part 1

Rules the computed answers use are listed in the subject’s library tests; every authored answer is in Part 2.

### `e5-2-terms` · Lesson 2 · multiple choice, level 1

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which term means a classification based on a word’s function in context? | word class in use | adjective · silent-e change · consonant doubling | That concerns a modifier of a noun in the model, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context. · That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context. · That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context. | The noun and verb uses show why context matters. |
| Which term means a modifier of a noun in the model? | adjective | word class in use · silent-e change · consonant doubling | That concerns a classification based on a word’s function in context, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model. · That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model. · That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model. | It describes the noun rather than carrying the clause’s tense. |
| Which term means dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model? | silent-e change | word class in use · adjective · consonant doubling | That concerns a classification based on a word’s function in context, rather than dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model. · That concerns a modifier of a noun in the model, rather than dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model. · That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model. | The computed model drops the final silent e. |
| Which term means the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan? | consonant doubling | word class in use · adjective · silent-e change | That concerns a classification based on a word’s function in context, rather than the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan. · That concerns a modifier of a noun in the model, rather than the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan. · That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan. | Doubling depends on the spelling and stress pattern, not a universal instruction. |

Right answer: `{X.r}`; wrong choices: `{X.w[0]}`, `{X.w[1]}`, `{X.w[2]}`

- Feedback `w0`: “{X.e[0]}”
- Feedback `w1`: “{X.e[1]}”
- Feedback `w2`: “{X.e[2]}”

Three generated variants:

> Which term means a modifier of a noun in the model?

- ✅ adjective
- ◻️ consonant doubling  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._
- ◻️ silent-e change  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._
- ◻️ word class in use  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a classification based on a word’s function in context, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._

Full working after a second miss:

> It describes the noun rather than carrying the clause’s tense.  
> So the answer is “adjective”.  

> Which term means a classification based on a word’s function in context?

- ◻️ consonant doubling  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context._
- ◻️ adjective  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a modifier of a noun in the model, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context._
- ✅ word class in use
- ◻️ silent-e change  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than a classification based on a word’s function in context._

Full working after a second miss:

> The noun and verb uses show why context matters.

> Which term means a modifier of a noun in the model?

- ◻️ consonant doubling  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._
- ◻️ silent-e change  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._
- ◻️ word class in use  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a classification based on a word’s function in context, rather than a modifier of a noun in the model._

### `e5-2-apply` · Lesson 2 · multiple choice, level 2

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Choose the spelling of the -ing form of write. | {ing("write")} | writeing · writting · writen | The silent final e is dropped in this model. · This verb does not double t in its -ing form. · That does not form the required -ing spelling. | Check the base and suffix rather than adding letters at random. |
| In a careful review, what is careful doing? | Describing the noun review. | Naming an action as the finite verb. · Replacing the whole subject as a personal pronoun. · Joining two clauses as a conjunction. | Review is modified by the adjective. · Careful does not replace the subject. · It does not join clauses here. | Classify the word in its sentence. |
| Which word is the verb in they record the decision? | record | they · the · decision | They is the subject pronoun. · The is a determiner. · Decision is a noun functioning within the object. | The same spelling record can have a different class in the record is clear. |
| Choose the regular past spelling of plan. | {past("plan")} | planed · planning · plans | The model doubles the final consonant before -ed. · This is the -ing form, not past tense. · This is a present singular form. | Apply the relevant spelling pattern, not every rule to every verb. |

### `e5-5-terms` · Lesson 5 · multiple choice, level 1

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which term means a detail directly supplied in the passage? | explicit information | supported inference · proposal · limit of evidence | That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. | The detail must actually be present. |
| Which term means a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue? | supported inference | explicit information · proposal · limit of evidence | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. | The conclusion needs an appropriate clue. |
| Which term means an action suggested rather than necessarily completed? | proposal | explicit information · supported inference · limit of evidence | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. · That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. | Intention and result are different. |
| Which term means what this passage cannot establish? | limit of evidence | explicit information · supported inference · proposal | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than what this passage cannot establish. · That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than what this passage cannot establish. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than what this passage cannot establish. | The evidence does not carry that universal scope. |

### `e5-8-terms` · Lesson 8 · multiple choice, level 1

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which term means a detail directly supplied in the passage? | explicit information | supported inference · proposal · limit of evidence | That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage. | The detail must actually be present. |
| Which term means a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue? | supported inference | explicit information · proposal · limit of evidence | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. | The conclusion needs an appropriate clue. |
| Which term means an action suggested rather than necessarily completed? | proposal | explicit information · supported inference · limit of evidence | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. · That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. · That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. | Intention and result are different. |
| Which term means what this passage cannot establish? | limit of evidence | explicit information · supported inference · proposal | That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than what this passage cannot establish. · That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than what this passage cannot establish. · That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than what this passage cannot establish. | The evidence does not carry that universal scope. |

- Feedback `w1`: “{X.e[1]}”
- Feedback `w2`: “{X.e[2]}”

- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._

### `e5-9-repair` · Lesson 9 · spot the error, level 3

Prompt: Find the incorrect claim or language form.

Shows 3 correct and 1 wrong statement(s), drawn from:

- ✅ definition clue: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression.
- ✅ contrast clue: opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning.
- ✅ collocation: a conventional combination of words.
- ✅ supported inference: a conclusion linked to clues actually provided.
- ❌ A definition clue requires no explanatory wording.  
  _↳ The nearby explanation supplies the meaning._
- ❌ A contrast always means the words are synonyms.  
  _↳ Opposition can distinguish the relevant properties._
- ❌ Any synonym can replace any word in a collocation naturally.  
  _↳ Conventional combinations constrain the choice._
- ❌ A guess with no textual clue is automatically proven.  
  _↳ An inference must connect to evidence in the passage._

| Which term means a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair? | voicing contrast | bilabial stop · alveolar stop · fricative | That concerns a consonant made with lip closure in the model, rather than a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair. · That concerns a stop at the alveolar place in the studied pair, rather than a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair. · That concerns a consonant with airflow through a narrow opening, rather than a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair. | Letters do not automatically specify one articulatory difference. |
| Which term means a consonant made with lip closure in the model? | bilabial stop | voicing contrast · alveolar stop · fricative | That concerns a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model. · That concerns a stop at the alveolar place in the studied pair, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model. · That concerns a consonant with airflow through a narrow opening, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model. | That describes the labiodental model, not bilabial closure. |

The notice is ready. | The room is open; the notice is ready. | The room is open, the notice is ready. · The room; is open the notice is ready. · The room is open the notice is ready. | A comma alone creates the comma splice being repaired. · That breaks the subject from its verb. · The clauses lack a boundary. | A semicolon connects the related complete clauses. |
| Use singular possession: the notebook belonging to Bih. | Bih’s notebook | Bihs notebook · Bihs’ notebook · Bih notebook’s | The possessive marker is missing. · That placement does not mark the named singular owner. · The marker belongs on the owner. | Mark the singular owner. |
| Choose the complete direct-quotation pattern. | Bih said, “The room is ready.” | Bih said, “The room is ready. · Bih, said The room is ready. · Bih said, The room is ready. | The closing quotation mark is missing. · This splits subject and verb and omits quotation marks. · The requested direct quotation needs speech marks. | The quotation needs paired marks. |
| Choose a clearly punctuated list. | Bring a pen, a notebook and a folder. | Bring a pen a notebook and a folder. · Bring, a pen a notebook and a folder. · Bring a, pen a, notebook and a folder. | Separate the first list item. · The comma splits verb and object rather than items. · These commas split determiners from nouns. | Separate items, not their internal noun groups. |

Right answer: `{X.r}`; wrong choices: `{X.w[0]}`, `{X.w[1]}`, `{X.w[2]}`

### `e5-20-terms` · Lesson 20 · multiple choice, level 1

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which term means the perspective presenting events? | narrative viewpoint | complication · causal development · resolution and reflection | That concerns a problem motivating consequential action, rather than the perspective presenting events. · That concerns events linked through choices and consequences, rather than the perspective presenting events. · That concerns an ending showing change and significance, rather than the perspective presenting events. | The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds. |

### `e5-20-repair` · Lesson 20 · spot the error, level 3

- ✅ causal development: events linked through choices and consequences.
- ✅ resolution and reflection: an ending showing change and significance.
- ❌ A first-person narrator automatically knows every private thought.  
  _↳ The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds._

  _↳ The complication motivates relevant action._

  _↳ Choices and consequences link events._

| The exercise asks for past reporting with backshift: Bih said, “I am ready.” Choose the matching report. | Bih said that she was ready. | Bih said that I am ready, with Bih still the referent of I. · Bih said that she were ready. · Bih said that she will ready. | The report changes the speaker reference and the task explicitly asks for backshift. · The ordinary singular reported statement uses was. · Will ready lacks the required be and changes the target. | Keep reference and the requested reporting perspective. |
| Backshift the completed statement under a past reporting frame: Ayuk said, “I have written the note.” | Ayuk said that he had written the note. | Ayuk said that he had wrote the note. · Ayuk said that he has writing the note. · Ayuk said that she had written the note, with she still referring to Ayuk. | Had takes the participle written. · The perfect needs a participle, not this -ing form. · The pronoun does not preserve the stated reference. | Use had plus participle and preserve the speaker. |
| A speaker said yesterday, “I will return tomorrow.” Report it later, from a different day, with ordinary backshift. | The speaker said they would return the next day. | The speaker said they would returned the next day. · The speaker said they will returning yesterday. · The speaker said tomorrow must keep exactly the same date in every report. | Would takes the base return. · The verb group and time reference are inconsistent. · The relative time word depends on the reporting viewpoint. | Both modal and time expression follow the changed frame. |
| Which claim about sequence of tenses is accurate? | Backshift can be unnecessary when the reported information is still true or relevant. | Every report must shift every verb regardless of context. · Past perfect must always shift to a new invented tense. · A reporting verb makes all pronouns meaningless. | Context can support retaining a present form. · Past perfect normally stays unchanged in the taught model. · References still need to be preserved. | Do not turn a useful reporting pattern into an absolute rule. |

- Feedback `w1`: “{X.e[1]}”

| Which term means a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future? | first-condition model | zero-condition model · second-condition model · third-condition model | That concerns a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship, rather than a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future. · That concerns an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future, rather than a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future. · That concerns an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past, rather than a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future. | Use present in the if-clause and will plus base in the result here. |

### `e5-22-apply` · Lesson 22 · multiple choice, level 2

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Choose the ordinary possible-future model. | If Bih arrives early, she will help. | If Bih will arrives early, she will help. · If Bih arrive early, she will helped. · If Bih arrived yesterday, she will helped yesterday. | The ordinary if-clause uses present, with correct agreement. · Arrives and help are required in this pattern. · This mixes incompatible time and verb patterns. | If-present links to will plus base. |
| Choose the imagined present/future model. | If Ayuk had more time, he would read. | If Ayuk had more time, he would reads. · If Ayuk has more time, he would to read. · If Ayuk had more time, he will reading. | Use base read after would. · Would does not take to in this pattern. · Will reading does not form the requested model. | If-past plus would-base fits the stated imagined situation. |
| Choose the unreal-past model. | If Bih had left earlier, she would have arrived on time. | If Bih had leave earlier, she would have arrived on time. · If Bih had left earlier, she would have arrive on time. · If Bih had left earlier, she would has arrived on time. | Had takes the participle left. · Have takes the participle arrived. · Would is followed by base have. | Both perfect patterns need the appropriate participles. |
| Which reformulation preserves the conditional relationship? | We will meet if the room is free. | The room is free if we will meet, with the same asserted condition. · We will meet although the room is free, with identical meaning. · The room was free because we met, with identical meaning. | That changes the condition’s direction and form. · Although expresses concession, not the same condition. · That invents past time and a causal relationship. | Moving the if-clause does not reverse which event is the condition. |

Right answer: `{X.r}`; wrong choices: `{X.w[0]}`, `{X.w[1]}`, `{X.w[2]}`

- Feedback `w0`: “{X.e[0]}”
- Feedback `w1`: “{X.e[1]}”
- Feedback `w2`: “{X.e[2]}”

Three generated variants:

> Choose the unreal-past model.

- ◻️ If Bih had leave earlier, she would have arrived on time.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Had takes the participle left._
- ✅ If Bih had left earlier, she would have arrived on time.
- ◻️ If Bih had left earlier, she would have arrive on time.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Have takes the participle arrived._
- ◻️ If Bih had left earlier, she would has arrived on time.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Would is followed by base have._

Full working after a second miss:

> Both perfect patterns need the appropriate participles.  
> So the answer is “If Bih had left earlier, she would have arrived on time.”.  

> Choose the ordinary possible-future model.

- ◻️ If Bih arrived yesterday, she will helped yesterday.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This mixes incompatible time and verb patterns._
- ◻️ If Bih will arrives early, she will help.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The ordinary if-clause uses present, with correct agreement._
- ✅ If Bih arrives early, she will help.
- ◻️ If Bih arrive early, she will helped.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Arrives and help are required in this pattern._

Full working after a second miss:

> If-present links to will plus base.  
> So the answer is “If Bih arrives early, she will help.”.  

> Choose the imagined present/future model.

- ◻️ If Ayuk had more time, he would reads.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Use base read after would._
- ◻️ If Ayuk had more time, he will reading.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Will reading does not form the requested model._
- ◻️ If Ayuk has more time, he would to read.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Would does not take to in this pattern._
- ✅ If Ayuk had more time, he would read.

Full working after a second miss:

> If-past plus would-base fits the stated imagined situation.  
> So the answer is “If Ayuk had more time, he would read.”.

### `e5-24-apply` · Lesson 24 · multiple choice, level 2

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Choose the quantifier for countable plural proposals: There are ___ proposals to compare. | several | much · a little · an | Much normally modifies uncountable quantity here. · A little does not fit these plural countable proposals. · An does not determine a plural noun. | Proposals are countable in this use. |
| Choose the uncountable pattern: We need ___ information. | a little more | a few more · many more · an extra | A few goes with plural countable items. · Many does not fit uncountable information in this use. · An would need a countable unit such as item. | Use an amount expression with information. |
| A few members attended, so some were present. Which reformulation preserves that meaning? | Some members attended, though the number was small. | No member attended. · All members definitely attended. · The phrase gives the exact attendance. | A few has a positive some meaning. · The statement does not say all. · No exact count is supplied. | Distinguish a small positive number from absence. |
| What does fairly in a fairly clear proposal contribute? | It qualifies the degree of clear. | It counts the exact proposals. · It is the main finite verb. · It necessarily means completely unclear. | It modifies the degree rather than stating a count. · The phrase does not make fairly a finite verb. · The qualifier does not reverse clear into that absolute opposite. | Qualifiers and quantifiers do different work in this context. |

Right answer: `{X.r}`; wrong choices: `{X.w[0]}`, `{X.w[1]}`, `{X.w[2]}`

- Feedback `w2`: “{X.e[2]}”

- ◻️ It counts the exact proposals.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It modifies the degree rather than stating a count._
- ◻️ It is the main finite verb.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The phrase does not make fairly a finite verb._
- ✅ It qualifies the degree of clear.

- ◻️ a few more  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A few goes with plural countable items._
- ◻️ many more  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Many does not fit uncountable information in this use._

Full working after a second miss:

- ◻️ positive small quantity: the some meaning of a few or a little in context.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the some meaning of a few or a little in context. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ degree qualifier: a modifier changing the strength of a description.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a modifier changing the strength of a description. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Much proposals is the required model.”.  
> Much does not fit this plural countable use.

### `e5-29-terms` · Lesson 29 · multiple choice, level 1

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which term means an element added before a base? | prefix | suffix · synonym in context · antonym in context | That concerns an element added after a base, rather than an element added before a base. · That concerns a word with a related meaning in the stated use, rather than an element added before a base. · That concerns a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect, rather than an element added before a base. | Its position is before the base. |
| Which term means an element added after a base? | suffix | prefix · synonym in context · antonym in context | That concerns an element added before a base, rather than an element added after a base. · That concerns a word with a related meaning in the stated use, rather than an element added after a base. · That concerns a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect, rather than an element added after a base. | Suffixes can alter class as well as meaning. |
| Which term means a word with a related meaning in the stated use? | synonym in context | prefix · suffix · antonym in context | That concerns an element added before a base, rather than a word with a related meaning in the stated use. · That concerns an element added after a base, rather than a word with a related meaning in the stated use. · That concerns a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect, rather than a word with a related meaning in the stated use. | Related meanings remain context-dependent. |
| Which term means a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect? | antonym in context | prefix · suffix · synonym in context | That concerns an element added before a base, rather than a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect. · That concerns an element added after a base, rather than a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect. · That concerns a word with a related meaning in the stated use, rather than a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect. | The opposition is semantic, not merely visual. |

### `e5-30-comprehend` · Lesson 30 · multiple choice, level 2

Prompt: {X.q}

Table `X` (one row is picked each time):

| q | r | w | e | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Which sources are mentioned? | Motor-vehicle combustion and dust. | Only harmless water vapour. · Only a completed laboratory result. · One guaranteed source for every place. | That replaces the named sources with an unsupported claim. · No result is supplied. · Examples do not establish a universal single source. | Use the stated examples. |
| What does the pupil propose recording? | Locations where visible smoke or dust is noticed. | A diagnosis for each pupil. · Exact concentrations without measurement. · The result of a completed survey. | No diagnosis is established. · Observation differs from measurement. · The survey is only proposed. | The proposal concerns observations. |
| Why does observation not establish concentration? | Noticing material differs from measuring pollutant concentration. | Every pollutant is visible. · Exact instrument readings are supplied. · Every proposal is a measurement. | Visibility does not establish every pollutant. · No readings are given. · Planning and measuring differ. | Match a claim to the method. |
